The '''2010 United States Senate elections''' were held on November 2, 2010, from among the [[United States Senate]]'s 100 seats. A special election was held on January 19, 2010, for a [[2010 United States Senate special election in Massachusetts|midterm vacancy in Massachusetts]]. 34 of the November elections were for 6-year terms to the [[Classes of United States senators|Senate's Class 3]], while other 3 were special elections to finish incomplete terms. Those 37 November elections featured 19 incumbent Democrats (7 of whom retired or were defeated in the primary) and 18 incumbent Republicans (8 of whom retired or were defeated in the primary).

After the [[2008 United States Senate elections|2008 elections]], the Senate was composed of 58 [[Democratic Party (United States)|Democrats]], 40 [[Republican Party (United States)|Republicans]], and 2 [[Independent politician|independents]] who caucused with the Democrats. However, in a January 2010 special election, Republicans picked up a seat in [[2010 United States Senate special election in Massachusetts|Massachusetts]] thereby reducing Democrats' majority to 57 seats.

Republicans won 4 seats held by retiring Democrats and also defeated 2 incumbent Democrats, for a Republican net gain of 6 seats. This was the first time since [[1994 United States Senate elections|1994]] that Republicans successfully defended all of their own seats. This was also the fourth consecutive election of Class 3 senators where Democrats failed to gain seats. Despite the Republican gains, the Democrats retained a majority of the Senate with 51 seats plus the 2 Independents who caucused with them, compared to the 47 Republican seats. {{As of|2025}}, this was the last time Republicans won U.S. Senate seats in [[2010 United States Senate elections in Illinois|Illinois]] and [[2010 United States Senate election in New Hampshire|New Hampshire]].

== Final pre-election predictions ==
Several sites and individuals publish predictions of competitive seats. These predictions look at factors such as the strength of the [[incumbent]] (if the incumbent is running for reelection) and the other candidates, and the state's partisan lean (reflected in part by the state's [[Cook Partisan Voting Index]] rating). The predictions assign ratings to each seat, indicating the predicted advantage that a party has in winning that seat.

Most election predictors used:
* "<u>tossup</u>": no advantage
* "<u>tilt</u>" (used by some predictors): advantage that is not quite as strong as "lean"
* "<u>lean</u>": slight advantage
* "<u>likely" or "favored</u>": significant, but surmountable, advantage
* "<u>safe" or "solid</u>": near-certain chance of victory

Where a site gives a percentage probability as its primary indicator of expected outcome, the chart below classifies a race as follows:
* Tossup: 50-55%
* Tilt: 56-60%
* Lean: 61-75%
* Likely: 76-93%
* Safe: 94-100%

===Retirements===

| map_caption = County results<br>'''Shelby''': {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}}  {{legend0|#c21b18|80–90%}}<br/>'''Barnes''': {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}} {{legend0|#584cde|70–80%}} {{legend0|#3933e5|80–90%}}

| title              = U.S. Senator
| before_election    = [[Richard Shelby]]
| before_party       = Republican Party (US)
| after_election     = [[Richard Shelby]]
| after_party        = Republican Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Alabama}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Alabama}}
Incumbent Republican [[Richard Shelby]] won re-election to a fifth term. On November 9, 1994, Shelby switched his party affiliation from Democratic to Republican, one day after the Republicans won control of both houses in the midterm elections, giving the Republicans a 53–47 majority in the Senate. He won his first full term as a Republican in 1998 by a large margin, and faced no significant opposition in 2004 and 2010.

Shelby had over $17 million in the bank, one of the highest of any candidate in the country. He had become even more popular in his opposition to the [[Troubled Asset Relief Program]] (TARP) and the [[Emergency Economic Stabilization Act of 2008]], as the ranking member of the [[Senate Banking Committee]].

In May, Shelby told reporters "I don't even know who my opponent is."<ref>{{Cite web |date=May 28, 2010 |title=Smooth sailing for Alabama's Shelby - MarketWatch's Election Blog - MarketWatch |url=http://blogs.marketwatch.com/election/2010/05/28/smooth-sailing-for-alabamas-shelby |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20110714041127/http://blogs.marketwatch.com/election/2010/05/28/smooth-sailing-for-alabamas-shelby/ |archive-date=July 14, 2011 |access-date=June 14, 2010 |publisher=Blogs.marketwatch.com}}</ref>

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Alaska}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Alaska}}
The November general election in Alaska was preceded by [[Partisan primary|primary election]]s which were held August 24, 2010. [[Scott McAdams]], the Mayor of [[Sitka, Alaska|Sitka]], became the Democratic nominee and [[Joe Miller (Alaska politician)|Joe Miller]], an attorney and former federal magistrate, became the Republican nominee after defeating incumbent Senator [[Lisa Murkowski]]<ref name="it">{{Cite news |last=Sean Cockerham |date=August 31, 2010 |title=It's another Tea Party win as Alaska's Murkowski concedes |work=[[Anchorage Daily News]] |url=http://www.mcclatchydc.com/2010/08/31/99960/alaska-absentee-count-looks-like.html |url-status=dead |access-date=September 1, 2010 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100901191632/http://www.mcclatchydc.com/2010/08/31/99960/alaska-absentee-count-looks-like.html |archive-date=September 1, 2010}}</ref> in the Republican primary.<ref name="info">{{Cite web |title=Primary Election Information |url=http://www.elections.alaska.gov/ei_primary.php |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20101203153504/http://www.elections.alaska.gov/ei_primary.php |archive-date=December 3, 2010 |access-date=December 13, 2010 |publisher=State of Alaska, Division of Elections |df=mdy-all}}</ref>

Murkowski garnered more than 100,000 write-in votes in the general election, many{{clarify|date=October 2019}} of which were challenged by Miller for various errors including minor misspellings. The appeal was denied<ref name="triumphs">{{Cite news |date=November 17, 2010 |title=Murkowski triumphs in Alaska Senate race |publisher=The Seattle Times |url=https://www.seattletimes.com/seattle-news/politics/murkowski-triumphs-in-alaska-senate-race/ |access-date=November 18, 2010}}</ref> Even if all the challenged votes were thrown out, Murkowski would have still had a lead of over 2,100 votes.<ref name="judge rules">{{Cite news |last=Cockerham |first=Sean |date=December 10, 2010 |title=Judge rules against Miller in Senate suit |work=Anchorage Daily News |url=http://www.adn.com/2010/12/10/1598722/judge-rules-against-miller-in.html |url-status=dead |access-date=December 13, 2010 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20101214085724/http://www.adn.com/2010/12/10/1598722/judge-rules-against-miller-in.html |archive-date=December 14, 2010 |df=mdy-all}}</ref>

After spending over $20 million during the primaries, McCain still had more than $1million cash on hand after the primary election. Glassman criticized McCain on women's issues.<ref>{{Cite web |title=On Gender Equity Day, McCain's Dismal Record on Women's Issues Highlighted |url=http://www.rodneyglassman.com/2010/08/on-gender-equity-day-mccains-dismal-record-on-womens-issues-highlighted/ |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20101001011929/http://www.rodneyglassman.com/2010/08/on-gender-equity-day-mccains-dismal-record-on-womens-issues-highlighted/ |archive-date=October 1, 2010 |access-date=2010-08-31 |df=mdy-all}}</ref> In August 2010, Glassman released a TV advertisement called "Arizona First."<ref>{{Cite web |title=U.S. Senate Candidate Rodney Glassman Launches "Arizona First" Ad |url=http://www.rodneyglassman.com/2010/08/u-s-senate-candidate-rodney-glassman-launches-arizona-first-ad/ |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100817234858/http://www.rodneyglassman.com/2010/08/u-s-senate-candidate-rodney-glassman-launches-arizona-first-ad/ |archive-date=August 17, 2010 |access-date=2010-08-31 |df=mdy-all}}</ref>

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Arkansas}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Arkansas}}
Incumbent Democrat [[Blanche Lincoln]] ran for re-election to a third term, but lost to Republican nominee [[John Boozman]]. Boozman became the first Republican in [[1872 United States Senate elections|138 years]] to win the seat. Arkansas had previously only elected one Republican senator since the [[Reconstruction era of the United States|Reconstruction]], who was defeated after his first term in 2002 by [[Mark Pryor]].

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in California}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from California}}
Incumbent Democrat [[Barbara Boxer]] won re-election to a fourth term.

In 2009, Boxer was criticized for correcting a general who called her "ma'am". Brigadier General Michael Walsh was testifying on the Louisiana coastal restoration process in the wake of [[Hurricane Katrina]] and answered Boxer's query with "ma'am" when Boxer interrupted him. "Do me a favor," Boxer said. "can you say 'senator' instead of 'ma'am?'" "Yes, ma'am," Walsh interjected. "It's just a thing, I worked so hard to get that title, so I'd appreciate it. Thank you," she said. The Army's guide to protocol instructs service members to call members of the U.S. Senate "sir", "ma'am" or "senator".<ref>{{Cite web |date=June 19, 2009 |title=Sen. Boxer to officer: Don't call me ma'am |url=http://www.washingtontimes.com/news/2009/jun/19/no-maam-for-boxer |access-date=May 25, 2010 |work=Washington Times}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |date=June 19, 2009 |title=Sen. Boxer Offers No Apology for Rebuking Brigadier General Who Called Her 'Ma'am' |website=[[Fox News]] |url=https://www.foxnews.com/politics/sen-boxer-offers-no-apology-for-rebuking-brigadier-general-who-called-her-maam |access-date=December 21, 2017}}</ref> Fiorina used this incident prominently in campaign ads, as did [[David Zucker (filmmaker)|David Zucker]], who directed a humorous commercial for [[RightChange.com]] titled 'Call Me Senator.'  In February 2010, Carly Fiorina put out a campaign ad attacking Republican rival [[Tom Campbell (California politician)|Tom Campbell]] featuring a "[[Demon Sheep|demon sheep]]", creating international, mostly negative, publicity.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Garofoli |first=Joe |date=February 5, 2010 |title=Demon sheep' ad may be bad move, experts say |work=[[San Francisco Chronicle]] |url=https://www.sfgate.com/politics/article/Demon-sheep-ad-may-be-bad-move-experts-say-3274078.php |access-date=June 8, 2010 |url-status=live |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20160402082302/http://www.sfgate.com/politics/article/Demon-sheep-ad-may-be-bad-move-experts-say-3274078.php |archive-date=April 2, 2016}}</ref>

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Colorado}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Colorado}}
In December 2008, [[President-elect of the United States|President-elect]] [[Barack Obama]] selected incumbent U.S. Senator [[Ken Salazar]] to become [[United States Secretary of the Interior|U.S. Secretary of the Interior]].<ref>{{Cite news |last1=Osher |first1=Christopher N. |last2=Bunch |first2=Joey |date=December 15, 2008 |title=Salazar poised to head Interior |publisher=Denver Post |url=http://www.denverpost.com/breakingnews/ci_11234643 |access-date=December 15, 2008}}</ref> After Salazar resigned from his seat,<ref>{{Cite news |date=January 19, 2009 |title=Ken Salazar Resigns From Senate |agency=Associated Press |url=http://www.thedenverchannel.com/news/18509866/detail.html |access-date=January 20, 2009 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20090122210619/http://thedenverchannel.com/news/18509866/detail.html |archive-date=January 22, 2009}}</ref> Democratic [[Governor of Colorado|Governor]] [[Bill Ritter]] appointed [[Denver Public Schools]] [[Superintendent (education)|Superintendent]] [[Michael Bennet]] to fill the seat, who won election to his first full term.<ref name="post">{{Cite news |last=Crummy |first=Karen |date=January 2, 2009 |title=Michael Bennet chosen as next Senator |work=[[Denver Post]] |url=http://www.denverpost.com/breakingnews/ci_11355396 |access-date=January 2, 2009}}</ref><ref>{{Cite news |last=Johnson |first=Kirk |title=Michael Bennet News |work=The New York Times |url=http://topics.nytimes.com/top/reference/timestopics/people/b/michael_bennet/index.html}}</ref>

{{Election box begin no change

This was one of the most expensive elections in the 2010 cycle, with more than $50 million spent total, including over $35 million in outside spending.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Most Expensive Elections |url=https://www.opensecrets.org/overview/topraces.php?cycle=2010&display=currcandsout |access-date=May 12, 2020}}</ref> Conservative third party groups hammered Bennet for voting 92% of the time with the Democratic leadership, including voting for healthcare reform and the stimulus package.<ref>{{Cite web |date=October 1, 2010 |title=Bennet vs. Bennet—Claims to represent Colorado, but Votes for the Washington Agenda 92% of the Time - Buck For Colorado |url=http://www.buckforcolorado.com/bennet-vs-bennet%E2%80%94claims-represent-colorado-votes-washington-agenda-92-time |url-status=bot: unknown |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20101001010458/http://www.buckforcolorado.com/bennet-vs-bennet%E2%80%94claims-represent-colorado-votes-washington-agenda-92-time |archive-date=October 1, 2010 |access-date=December 21, 2017 |df=mdy-all}}</ref> Liberal third party groups called Buck extremist. Bennet focused on attacking Buck's views on abortion, which he believed should be banned including those of cases of [[rape]] and [[incest]]. He was also attacked for wanting to eliminate the [[Seventeenth Amendment to the United States Constitution|Seventeenth Amendment]]<ref>{{Cite web |last=Brady |first=Jeff |date=October 27, 2010 |title=Money Has Poured Into Colorado's Senate Race |url=https://www.npr.org/templates/story/story.php?storyId=130864318 |access-date=August 3, 2016 |website=npr.org}}</ref> and refusing to prosecute an alleged rapist as Weld County district attorney. [[Planned Parenthood]] mounted a mail campaign, targeting women voters with the warning that "Colorado women can't trust Ken Buck." Bennet won the women vote by 17 points according to exit polls. After the election, Buck conceded to the [[Denver Post]] that the main reason why he lost is because of social issues.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Burns |first=Alexander |date=November 13, 2010 |title=Abortion was winning issue for Dems |url=https://www.politico.com/story/2010/11/abortion-was-winning-issue-for-dems-045069 |access-date=August 3, 2016 |website=politico.com}}</ref>

Incumbent Democrat [[Christopher Dodd]] suffered from dropping approval ratings in the past few years due to major controversies, leading him to announce in January 2010 that he would retire, instead of seeking a sixth term.<ref>{{Cite news |title=Chris Dodd Announces Retirement, Says "None of Us is Irreplaceable" |work=[[CBS News]] |url=https://www.cbsnews.com/news/chris-dodd-announces-retirement-says-none-of-us-is-irreplaceable |access-date=December 15, 2017}}</ref> As Dodd was a Democrat, [[Richard Blumenthal]], incumbent [[Connecticut Attorney General|State Attorney General]], announced on the same day that he would run for Dodd's seat.<ref>{{Cite news |title=Christopher J. Dodd : Christopher J. Dodd News and Photos |publisher=Courant.com |url=http://www.courant.com/news/politics/hc-richard-blumenthal-chris-dodd-0106,0,2997693.story |url-status=dead |access-date=June 10, 2010 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100109115121/http://www.courant.com/news/politics/hc-richard-blumenthal-chris-dodd-0106,0,2997693.story |archive-date=January 9, 2010 |df=mdy-all}}</ref> The [[Connecticut Democratic Party]] nominated Blumenthal on May 21. Businesswoman [[Linda McMahon]] won the [[Connecticut Republican Party|state party's]] nominating convention and the August 10 Republican primary to become the Republican candidate.<ref name="CT_Primaries">{{Cite web |date=August 10, 2010 |title=Connecticut Primaries: Results |url=http://blogs.courant.com/capitol_watch/2010/08/connecticut-primaries-state-ho.html |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://archive.today/20120707160002/http://blogs.courant.com/capitol_watch/2010/08/connecticut-primaries-state-ho.html |archive-date=July 7, 2012 |access-date=August 12, 2010 |publisher=[[Hartford Courant]] |df=mdy-all}}</ref>

{{Main|2010 United States Senate special election in Delaware}}

This was a [[by-election|special election]] to fill Delaware's Class 2 Senate seat, then held by [[Ted Kaufman]], an appointee. The seat had been previously held by long-time Senator [[Joe Biden]], who vacated it when he became [[Vice President of the United States]] in 2009.

== Florida ==
{{Infobox election

| country            = Florida

| map_image         = 2010 United States Senate election in Florida results map by county.svg
| map_size          = 250px
| map_caption = 

'''Rubio:''' {{legend0|#ffc8cd|30–40%}} {{legend0|#ffb2b2|40–50%}} {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}} <br/>
'''Crist:''' {{legend0|#E6E6E6|30–40%}} {{legend0|#D9D9D9|40–50%}} <br/>'''Meek:''' {{legend0|#a5b0ff|40–50%}} {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}}

| title              = U.S. Senator
| before_election    = [[George LeMieux]]
| before_party       = Republican Party (US)
| after_election     = [[Marco Rubio]]
| after_party        = Republican Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Florida}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Florida}}
Incumbent Republican Senator [[Mel Martínez]], who was elected in a very close race against Democrat [[Betty Castor]] with 49% of the vote in [[2004 United States Senate election in Florida|2004]], announced on December 2, 2008, that he would not run for re-election to a second term,<ref>{{Cite news |last=[[Dana Bash]] and [[Ted Barrett]] |date=December 2, 2008 |title=Sen. Martinez won't seek reelection |publisher=[[CNN.com]] |url=http://politicalticker.blogs.cnn.com/2008/12/02/sen-martinez-wont-seek-reelection/?eref=politicalflipper |access-date=December 18, 2017 |archive-date=May 20, 2009 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20090520054854/http://politicalticker.blogs.cnn.com/2008/12/02/sen-martinez-wont-seek-reelection/?eref=politicalflipper |url-status=dead }}</ref> then announcing on August 7, 2009, that he would resign prior to the end of his term.<ref>{{Cite news |date=August 7, 2009 |title=First on the CNN Ticker: Florida senator to resign seat |work=CNN |url=http://politicalticker.blogs.cnn.com/2009/08/07/florida-senator-to-resign-seat |access-date=May 12, 2010 |archive-date=December 28, 2009 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20091228045014/http://politicalticker.blogs.cnn.com/2009/08/07/florida-senator-to-resign-seat/ |url-status=dead }}</ref> The [[Governor of Florida]], Republican [[Charlie Crist]], was required to appoint a successor and he chose his former Chief of Staff, [[George LeMieux]]. LeMieux, a [[Placeholder (politics)|placeholder]] who did not run in the election, replaced Martínez in the Senate on September 10, 2009.

Crist publicly announced he was running for the seat in mid-2009. When he declared his candidacy, he received many Republican endorsements, including the [[National Republican Senatorial Committee]], Martínez, and 2008 Republican presidential nominee [[John McCain]]. However, his support of the [[American Recovery and Reinvestment Act of 2009]] hurt his popularity among conservatives, and [[Tea Party movement|Tea Party]] candidate [[Marco Rubio]], the former [[Speaker of the Florida House of Representatives]], surged in the polls. In April 2010, Crist announced he would drop out of the Republican primary and run as an Independent. The National Republican Senatorial Committee withdrew its endorsement of Crist and demanded a refund of its campaign funds that it provided for the Crist campaign.<ref>{{Cite web |date=April 29, 2010 |title=If Crist leaves party, GOP donors vow to take cash back |url=http://www.washingtontimes.com/news/2010/apr/29/gop-donors-vow-to-take-cash-back-if-crist-leaves-p |access-date=June 14, 2010 |work=Washington Times}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |date=April 29, 2010 |title=Cornyn on Crist: I Want My Money Back - The Note |url=http://blogs.abcnews.com/thenote/2010/04/cornyn-on-crist-i-want-my-money-back.html |access-date=June 14, 2010 |publisher=Blogs.abcnews.com}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |date=February 18, 2010 |title=Marco Rubio on Charlie Crist Party Switch in Florida Senate Race - ABC News |url=https://abcnews.go.com/Nightline/Politics/marco-rubio-charlie-crist-party-switch-florida-senate/story?id=10508166&page=2 |access-date=June 14, 2010 |publisher=Abcnews.go.com}}</ref> Rubio went on to win the Republican primary against only token opposition.

Polling initially showed Crist neck and neck with Rubio, but by the end of August Rubio opened up a solid and consistent lead. He was supported by Republican and some Independent voters whereas Democratic and other Independents were split between Crist and Meek. Rubio went on to win the election with 49% of the vote to Crist's 30% and Meek's 20%.

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Hawaii}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Hawaii}}
Incumbent Democrat and [[President pro tempore of the United States Senate|President pro tempore]] [[Daniel Inouye]] won re-election to his ninth term.

Hawaii last elected a Republican Senator in 1970, and its current delegation to the [[United States Congress]] currently consists entirely of Democrats. Democrats have also won Hawaii's [[Electoral College (United States)|electoral votes]] in every [[United States presidential election|presidential election]] since [[Ronald Reagan]]'s [[1984 United States presidential election|landslide election in 1984]]. The exceptions at the time were then-[[Governor of Hawaii|Governor]] [[Linda Lingle]] (who was serving her second and final term) and then-[[United States House of Representatives|U.S. Representative]] [[Charles Djou]], both of whom were Republicans.

Incumbent Democrat [[Roland Burris]] did not run in 2010.<ref name="burris">{{Cite web |last=Pierce |first=Emily |date=July 9, 2009 |title=Burris Won't Run in 2010 |url=http://www.rollcall.com/news/36651-1.html |access-date=June 15, 2010 |website=[[Roll Call]]}}</ref> He suffered from poor approval ratings<ref>{{Cite web |date=May 21, 2009 |title=publicpolicypolling.blogspot.com |url=http://publicpolicypolling.blogspot.com/2009/05/updated-senate-approvals.html |access-date=June 14, 2010 |publisher=publicpolicypolling.blogspot.com}}</ref> and was investigated by the [[Sangamon County, Illinois]] State's Attorney for [[perjury]].<ref>{{Cite news |last=Korecki |first=Natasha |date=March 6, 2009 |title=suntimes.com |work=Chicago Sun-Times |url=http://www.suntimes.com/news/metro/burris/1463775,CST-NWS-invest06.article |url-status=dead |access-date=June 14, 2010 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20090418163320/http://www.suntimes.com/news/metro/burris/1463775,CST-NWS-invest06.article |archive-date=April 18, 2009 |df=mdy-all}}</ref> Although no criminal charges were filed against him, he faced an investigation by the [[Senate Ethics Committee]].<ref>{{Cite web |title=dailyherald.com |url=http://www.dailyherald.com/story/?id=301611 |access-date=June 14, 2010 |publisher=dailyherald.com}}</ref>

There were two ballot items for the same seat: a regular election, to fill the [[Classes of United States senators|Class 3 seat]] beginning with the [[112th United States Congress]] beginning on January 3, 2011, and a special election, to fill that seat for the final weeks of the [[111th United States Congress|111th Congress]], replacing the temporary appointment of [[Roland Burris]] to the vacancy created by [[Barack Obama]]'s election to the presidency.<ref name="triblocal-0811">{{Cite news |date=August 11, 2010 |title=Illinois Special Election for U.S. Senate Seat |work=[[Chicago Tribune|Triblocal]] |publisher=[[Tribune Company|Chicagoland Publishing Company, Inc.]] |url=http://triblocal.com/grayslake/2010/08/11/illinois-special-election-for-u-s-senate-seat |url-status=dead |access-date=August 12, 2010 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20110726123044/http://triblocal.com/grayslake/2010/08/11/illinois-special-election-for-u-s-senate-seat/ |archive-date=July 26, 2011}}</ref>

A [[United States District Court for the Northern District of Illinois|federal court]] ruled that the candidates appearing on the ballot for the replacement election would be the ones of the regular election,<ref name="triblocal-0811" /><ref name="ap-0802">{{Cite news |date=August 2, 2010 |title=Judge confirms same ballots in Ill. Sen. elections |agency=[[The Associated Press]] |url=https://www.google.com/hostednews/ap/article/ALeqM5gofdG18qVRuAQKRIGyv7oeKLS9cgD9HBM9101}}{{dead link|date=June 2024|bot=medic}}{{cbignore|bot=medic}}</ref> and that the special election would appear after the regular election on the ballot.<ref name="ap-0802" />

{{As of|2026}}, this was the last Senate election in Illinois won by a Republican.

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Indiana}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Indiana}}
Incumbent Democrat [[Evan Bayh]] decided in February 2010 to retire instead of seeking a third term shortly after former U.S. Senator [[Dan Coats]] announced his candidacy for Bayh's contested seat.<ref name="bayhout">{{Cite news |last=Fritze |first=John |date=February 15, 2010 |title=Indiana Sen. Evan Bayh Drops Out |publisher=[[USA Today]] |url=http://content.usatoday.com/communities/onpolitics/post/2010/02/indiana-sen-evan-bayh-drops-out/1 |access-date=February 15, 2010}}</ref> No Democratic candidate submitted enough signatures by the deadline to run, leading Democratic officials to choose U.S. Congressman [[Brad Ellsworth]] to be the nominee. Coats won the election.

After Coats's win in the Republican primary, Ellsworth began to heavily criticize Coats for his ties to lobbyists. He called for more disclosure of the meetings lawmakers have with lobbyists, banning congressional staff from lobbying for six years after their congressional jobs, requiring Congress members to put all their investments in blind trusts, more disclosure of Senate candidates' personal financial information, and changes to the U.S. Senate filibuster rules. He proposed lowering number of votes required to break a filibuster to 55 from the current 60.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Brad Ellsworth {{!}} Ellsworth for Indiana U.S. Senate Campaign {{!}} News |url=http://www.ellsworthforindiana2010.com/news |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100810182146/http://www.ellsworthforindiana2010.com/news |archive-date=August 10, 2010 |access-date=August 21, 2010 |publisher=Ellsworthforindiana2010.com |df=mdy-all}}</ref> In response to Ellsworth's charges, Coats published his lobbying record in an 815-page document.

Coats emphasized the individual issues rather than ethics reforms advocated by his opponent. He focused on Ellsworth's record of voting in support of the [[Economic Stimulus Act of 2008]], cap-and-trade legislation, and [[Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act|health care bill]]. Coats opinion of the healthcare law was that "the only responsible solution ... is to repeal the Obama-Pelosi-Ellsworth health spending bill and quickly replace it with cost-effective, incremental pieces that will decrease costs, increase coverage and not break the bank."

}}

Incumbent [[Chuck Grassley]] started the campaign moderately popular, but his approval ratings dropped somewhat during the campaign.<ref>{{Cite web |title=IA Sr Sen Approval |url=http://www.surveyusa.com/client/PollTrack.aspx?g=8bf1ca92-79c8-463f-868b-b4ea297f6acf |access-date=June 14, 2010 |publisher=SurveyUSA}}</ref> However, the seat continued to be considered to be "Safe Republican" by many sources, with CQ Politics noting that Grassley is "one of Iowa's most durable politicians."

Conlin described herself as a "prairie progressive." She supported the recent landmark case of [[Varnum v. Brien]], which legalized gay marriage in the state. She also supported repeal of "[[don't ask, don't tell]]."<ref>{{Cite web |title=LGBT Issues {{!}} Roxanne Conlin for Iowa |url=http://roxanneforiowa.com/index.php/pages/lgbt-issues |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100812170340/http://roxanneforiowa.com/index.php/pages/lgbt-issues/ |archive-date=August 12, 2010 |access-date=August 21, 2010 |publisher=Roxanneforiowa.com |df=mdy-all}}</ref>

Before the election, former political advisor John Maxwell claimed that Grassley would have his toughest race since his first U.S. Senate election in [[1980 United States Senate election in Iowa|1980]], where he defeated incumbent [[John Culver]] with 53% of the vote. Grassley won all of his four re-election bids with nearly 70% of the vote against unknown opponents.<ref>{{Cite web |date=June 11, 2010 |title=Analysts: Roxanne Conlin likely to be Chuck Grassley's top rival since '80 {{!}} Des Moines Register Staff Blogs |url=http://blogs.desmoinesregister.com/dmr/index.php/2010/06/11/analysts-roxanne-conlin-likely-to-be-chuck-grassleys-top-rival-since-80 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://archive.today/20120707204304/http://blogs.desmoinesregister.com/dmr/index.php/2010/06/11/analysts-roxanne-conlin-likely-to-be-chuck-grassleys-top-rival-since-80/ |archive-date=July 7, 2012 |access-date=August 21, 2010 |publisher=Blogs.desmoinesregister.com |df=mdy-all}}</ref> Grassley won the election with 64.51% of the vote.

Incumbent Republican [[Sam Brownback]] retired to [[2010 Kansas gubernatorial election|run for Governor of Kansas]], instead of seeking a third term. Republican nominee [[Jerry Moran]] won the open seat. Kansas is one of the most Republican states in the nation;<ref>{{Cite web |date=2011-12-04 |title=Obama to give economic talk in Kansas - CBS News |url=https://www.cbsnews.com/news/obama-to-give-economic-talk-in-kansas/ |access-date=2024-06-03 |website=www.cbsnews.com |language=en-US}}</ref> no Democrat has been elected to either Senate seat since 1932.

The retirement of Brownback, a popular U.S. Senator, led to a heavily competitive primary election. Tiahrt, who was on the [[United States House Committee on Appropriations|Committee of Appropriations]], had been accused of excessive earmarking while he was in Congress. From 2006 to 2008, Tiahrt had requested and supported a total of 63 solo earmarks, costing $53.9 million. In the same period, Moran had requested and supported a total of 29 earmarks, with a pricetag of $13.4 million.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Clark |first=Matthew |date=June 5, 2010 |title=Politics Blog: Earmarks, earmarks and more earmarks - Pittsburg, KS |url=http://www.morningsun.net/lifestyles/patricks_people/x157348935/POLITICS-BLOG-Earmarks-earmarks-and-more-earmarks |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20110726131710/http://www.morningsun.net/lifestyles/patricks_people/x157348935/POLITICS-BLOG-Earmarks-earmarks-and-more-earmarks |archive-date=July 26, 2011 |access-date=August 21, 2010 |publisher=Morning Sun |df=mdy-all}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |date=June 7, 2010 |title=Truth Test |url=https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-2Y_BRB52Bg |access-date=August 21, 2010 |publisher=YouTube}}{{cbignore}}{{Dead YouTube link|date=February 2022}}</ref>

After the primaries, Moran chose not to release any more negative advertisements. Democrat Lisa Johnston ran a low-profile, quiet race. On election day, she won only two counties: [[Wyandotte County, Kansas|Wyandotte County]] and [[Douglas County, Kansas|Douglas County]], while Moran won statewide by a landslide.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Wyandotte County voters buck statewide trend - BonnerSprings.com |url=http://www.bonnersprings.com/news/2010/nov/02/democrats-take-lead-wyandotte-county/ |access-date=December 21, 2017 |website=www.bonnersprings.com |archive-date=November 13, 2010 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20101113000739/http://www.bonnersprings.com/news/2010/nov/02/democrats-take-lead-wyandotte-county/ |url-status=dead }}</ref>

Incumbent Republican [[Jim Bunning]] retired instead of seeking a third term. Republican nominee [[Rand Paul]] won the open seat.<ref name="primarywins">{{Cite news |last=Brammer |first=Jack |date=May 18, 2010 |title=Paul wins Kentucky GOP Senate nomination, to face Conway |publisher=McClatchy Newspapers |url=http://www.kentucky.com/2010/05/19/1270661/paul-wins-kentucky-gop-senate.html |access-date=May 20, 2010 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100522125333/http://www.kentucky.com/2010/05/19/1270661/paul-wins-kentucky-gop-senate.html |archive-date=May 22, 2010}}</ref>

On May 18, 2010, Paul won the Republican nomination.<ref name="primarywins" /> After conceding the election to Paul, Grayson said, "It's time to put all differences aside, unite behind Dr. Paul, he needs our help and I for one stand ready to serve".<ref>Cate Slattery. (May 18, 2010) [http://wtvq.com/news/3620-grayson-concedes-early- "Grayson Concedes Early"] {{webarchive | url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100522184507/http://wtvq.com/news/3620-grayson-concedes-early- | date=May 22, 2010 }} [[WTVQ]] News. Retrieved May 19, 2010.</ref>

Grayson attacked Paul for his "strange ideas," such as his opposition of the [[PATRIOT Act]], and what Grayson alleged to be his support of closing down [[Guantanamo Bay detention camp|Guantanamo Bay]] and saying that [[Iran]] was not a threat. He also attacked Paul for being a [[Duke University]] fan.{{Relevance inline|date=August 2022}} He sent out another TV ad and web video that stirred controversy by making the case that Paul believes that foreign policy decisions made prior to September 11, 2001, are partially to blame for the attacks.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Friends of Trey Grayson :: Media Gallery :: Videos |url=http://www.treygrayson.com/media-gallery/videos |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100501202553/http://www.treygrayson.com/media-gallery/videos |archive-date=May 1, 2010}}</ref> Paul immediately responded by launching a statewide television ad in which he expresses his "outrage at terrorists who killed 3,000 innocents" before accusing Grayson of a "lie" and a "shameful" tactic.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Cillizza |first=Chris |title=Trey Grayson, Rand Paul and the politics of September 11, 2001 |publisher=Voices.washingtonpost.com |url=http://voices.washingtonpost.com/thefix/senate/trey-grayson-rand-paul-and-the.html?wprss=thefix |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100710014127/http://voices.washingtonpost.com/thefix/senate/trey-grayson-rand-paul-and-the.html?wprss=thefix |url-status=dead |archive-date=July 10, 2010 |access-date=June 14, 2010}}</ref> Grayson accused the [[Fox News Channel]] of favoring Paul over him.

Conway began the race trailing Paul, but as he attacked his opponent's positions on social-welfare and criminal-justice policies, the polls began to tighten. The campaign attracted $8.5 million in contributions from outside groups, of which $6 million was spent to help Rand Paul and $2.5 million to help Conway. This money influx was in addition to the money spent by the candidates themselves: $6 million by Paul and $4.7 million by Conway.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Steitzer |first=Stephenie |date=October 29, 2010 |title="Outside groups spend big in U.S. Senate race" (October 29, 2010) Louisville Courier-Journal |url=http://www.courier-journal.com/article/20101029/NEWS0106/310290083 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://archive.today/20120731152912/http://www.courier-journal.com/article/20101029/NEWS0106/310290083 |archive-date=July 31, 2012 |access-date=May 8, 2012 |publisher=Courier-journal.com}}{{registration required}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |title="Outside spending – Kentucky Senate" (xxx) Sunlight Foundation |url=http://reporting.sunlightfoundation.com/outside-spending/race/KY-Senate |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120723051839/http://reporting.sunlightfoundation.com/outside-spending/race/KY-Senate |archive-date=July 23, 2012 |access-date=May 8, 2012 |publisher=Reporting.sunlightfoundation.com |df=mdy-all}}</ref>

Melançon heavily criticized Vitter for the [[Deborah Jeane Palfrey|prostitution sex scandal]].<ref>{{Cite web |title=Melancon's First TV Ad: 'David Vitter Hasn't Been Honest With Louisiana' (VIDEO) |url=http://tpmdc.talkingpointsmemo.com/2010/08/melancons-first-tv-ad-david-vitter-hasnt-been-honest-with-louisiana-video.php |access-date=December 21, 2017 |archive-date=August 18, 2010 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100818000049/http://tpmdc.talkingpointsmemo.com/2010/08/melancons-first-tv-ad-david-vitter-hasnt-been-honest-with-louisiana-video.php |url-status=dead }}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |title=Melancon Turns Vitter Prostitutution Reenactment Video Into Two-Minute TV Ad (VIDEO) |url=http://tpmdc.talkingpointsmemo.com/2010/09/melancon-turns-vitter-prostitute-reenactment-video-into-two-minute-tv-ad.php |access-date=December 21, 2017 }}{{Dead link|date=August 2026 |bot=InternetArchiveBot }}</ref> Vitter released television advertising criticizing Melancon for his support for Obama's stimulus package and his support for [[amnesty]] for [[Illegal immigrant population of the United States|illegal immigrants]].<ref>{{Cite web |title=David Vitter slams Charlie Melancon in new Senate TV ad |url=http://www.nola.com/politics/index.ssf/2010/08/david_vitter_slams_charlie_mel.html |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100815011011/http://www.nola.com/politics/index.ssf/2010/08/david_vitter_slams_charlie_mel.html |url-status=dead |archive-date=August 15, 2010 |access-date=December 21, 2017}}</ref>  Melancon claimed "In August, Melancon challenged Vitter to a series of five live, televised town hall-style debates across the state. In his 2004 campaign for Senate, Vitter committed to five live, televised debates. Since Melancon issued the challenge, Vitter and Melancon have been invited to a total of seven live, televised debates. Vitter only accepted invitations to debates hosted by WWL-TV and WDSU-TV, both in New Orleans.<ref>{{Cite web |title = Louisiana Politics: Vitter Agrees To Second Televised Debate In New Orleans  |url=http://www.bayoubuzz.com/buzz/latest-buzz/82514-louisiana-politics-vitter-agrees-to-second-televised-debate-in-new-orleans |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120313221513/http://www.bayoubuzz.com/buzz/latest-buzz/82514-louisiana-politics-vitter-agrees-to-second-televised-debate-in-new-orleans |archive-date=March 13, 2012 |access-date=December 18, 2017 |df=mdy-all}}</ref>

Republican nominee Eric Wargotz, [[Queen Anne's County, Maryland]] Commission President and physician, compared Mikulski to a [[dinosaur]] by calling her a political "insidersaurus" for being in Washington for over thirty years<ref>{{Cite web|url=https://www.politico.com/blogs/ben-smith/2010/09/insidersaurus-028958|title = 'Insidersaurus'| website=[[Politico]] | date=September 2010 }}</ref><ref>{{Cite web|url=https://www.theatlantic.com/politics/archive/2010/09/senate-candidate-runs-on-dinosaur-metaphor/340166/|title = Senate Candidate Runs on Dinosaur Metaphor| website=[[The Atlantic]] |date = September 2, 2010}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web|url=https://www.realclearpolitics.com/video/2010/09/02/md-sen_wargotz_hits_mikulski_over_political_insidersaurus-comments.html|title=MD-Sen: Wargotz Hits Mikulski For Being A "Political Insidersaurus" &#124; RealClearPolitics|website=www.realclearpolitics.com|accessdate=February 11, 2024}}</ref> An ad showed a hammer hitting a brick wall, breaking it down and citing criticisms of Mikulski's record as a U.S. Senator. Mikulski released advertisements emphasizing education and job creation.<ref>{{Cite news |last1=Pershing |first1=Ben |last2=Dropp |first2=Kyle |date=October 4, 2010 |title=Mikulski leads despite drop in popularity |newspaper=The Washington Post |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/wp-dyn/content/article/2010/10/03/AR2010100304687_3.html?sid=ST2010100401599}}</ref>  Despite Wargotz's limited campaign and resources he received the highest percentage of votes against Mikulski as an incumbent U.S. Senator

== Massachusetts (special) ==
{{Infobox election

| map_image = {{Switcher

| Municipality results
| default=1}}
| map_size = 250px
| map_caption = '''Brown:''' {{legend0|#ffb2b2|40–50%}} {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#D72F30|70–80%}} <br/>'''Coakley:''' {{legend0|#A5B0FF|40–50%}} {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}} {{legend0|#584cde|70–80%}} <br/>'''Tie:''' {{legend0|#D2B1D9|40–50%}}
| title              = U.S. senator
| before_election    = [[Paul G. Kirk|Paul Kirk]]
| before_party       = Democratic Party (US)
| after_election     = [[Scott Brown (politician)|Scott Brown]]
| after_party        = Republican Party (US)

{{Main|2010 United States Senate special election in Massachusetts}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Massachusetts}}
A special election was held January 19, 2010 to fill the Class 1 seat for the remainder of the term ending January 3, 2013. It was won by Republican [[Scott Brown (politician)|Scott Brown]].

Incumbent Democrat [[Ted Kennedy]] had died on August 25, 2009.  [[Governor of Massachusetts]] appointed Democrat [[Paul G. Kirk|Paul Kirk]] September 24, 2009, to continue the term pending this special election, in which Kirk was not a candidate.

The election was viewed by conservatives outside of Massachusetts as a [[referendum]] on President [[Barack Obama]].<ref>{{Cite news |last=Nasaw |first=Daniel |date=2010-01-17 |title=Big guns back flagging Democrats for Ted Kennedy seat |url=https://www.theguardian.com/world/2010/jan/17/obama-massachusetts-coakley-campaign |access-date=2024-06-03 |work=The Guardian |language=en-GB |issn=0261-3077}}</ref> However, Brown stated that he didn't believe that it was a referendum on Obama.<ref name="Referendum">{{Cite news |date=January 20, 2010 |title=Brown: Mass. Win about Anger, not Obama |work=CBS News |agency=Associated Press |url=https://www.cbsnews.com/news/brown-mass-win-about-anger-not-obama/ |access-date=October 7, 2013}}</ref>  Although Democrats would retain control of both Houses of Congress until January 2011, Brown's victory greatly affected their political plans, most notably for the [[Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act]], though the legislation was signed into law two months later.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Obama's Deal – FRONTLINE – PBS |url=https://www.pbs.org/wgbh/pages/frontline/obamasdeal/ |website=www.pbs.org}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |date=March 24, 2010 |title=Scott Brown and Buyer's Remorse |url=https://www.outsidethebeltway.com/scott_brown_and_buyers_remorse/}}</ref> Brown became the first Republican to win this seat since [[1946 United States Senate election|1946]], and {{as of|2026|lc=yes}}, this is the last Senate election in Massachusetts won by a Republican.

Reid was initially considered vulnerable, with the non-partisan [[Cook Political Report]] rating the election as a toss-up<ref>{{Cite web |date=September 10, 2009 |title=Reid's Seat Now Rated a Toss-Up |url=http://politicalwire.com/archives/2009/09/10/reids_seat_now_rated_a_toss_up.html |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20091231153323/http://politicalwire.com/archives/2009/09/10/reids_seat_now_rated_a_toss_up.html |archive-date=December 31, 2009 |access-date=June 14, 2010 |publisher=Politicalwire.com |df=mdy-all}}</ref> and the [[Rothenberg Political Report]] rating the state as toss-up.<ref>{{Cite web |date=February 24, 2010 |title=The Rothenberg Political Report: CA Senate moved to Clear Advantage from Safe |url=http://rothenbergpoliticalreport.blogspot.com/2010/02/ca-senate-moved-to-clear-advantage-from.html |access-date=June 14, 2010 |publisher=Rothenbergpoliticalreport.blogspot.com}}</ref> A June 9, 2010, [[Rasmussen Reports]] post-primary poll showed Angle leading incumbent Senator Harry Reid by a margin of 50% to 39%.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Election 2010: Nevada Senate polling |url=http://www.rasmussenreports.com/public_content/politics/elections/election_2010/election_2010_senate_elections/nevada/election_2010_nevada_senate |access-date=July 9, 2010 |publisher=Rasmussenreports.com}}</ref> However, a July 2010 poll showed Senator Reid leading Angle by 7 points, following nationwide attention to some of Angle's positions,<ref name="washingtonpost1">{{Cite news |date=July 16, 2010 |title=Harry Reid takes the lead over Sharron Angle in Nevada |newspaper=The Washington Post |url=http://voices.washingtonpost.com/thefix/morning-fix/1-2-colorado-republican-gubern.html|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20101016122033/http://voices.washingtonpost.com/thefix/morning-fix/1-2-colorado-republican-gubern.html|url-status=dead|archive-date=October 16, 2010}}</ref> as well as the endorsement of Reid by prominent Republicans. The change of margin, 18% in less than a month, is the largest in Senate elections history.<ref name="washingtonpost1" /> On July 28, 2010, [[Rasmussen Reports]] moved the race from toss-up to leans Democratic.<ref>{{Cite web |date=July 28, 2010 |title=Election 2010: Nevada Senate |url=http://www.rasmussenreports.com/public_content/politics/elections/election_2010/election_2010_senate_elections/nevada/election_2010_nevada_senate |access-date=July 28, 2010 |publisher=Rasmussenreports.com}}</ref> Later, it moved back to toss-up. Despite Angle leading by three points in the polls the days leading up to the election, Reid defeated her by 5.74%, even in her own county, [[Washoe County, Nevada|Washoe County]]. Reid also secured huge numbers out of the Democratic stronghold of [[Clark County, Nevada|Clark County]], which covers the Las Vegas Metropolitan Area. Angle's far right beliefs and positions were also widely cited as a reason for the failure of the Republican Party defeating Reid.<ref>{{Cite web|url=https://www.politico.com/story/2010/11/angles-campaign-sank-candidate-045463|title=Angle's campaign sank candidate|first=Shira|last=Toeplitz|date=November 21, 2010|website=POLITICO|accessdate=February 11, 2024}}</ref>

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in New Hampshire}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from New Hampshire}}
Incumbent Republican [[Judd Gregg]] retired instead of seeking a fourth term. Republican nominee [[Kelly Ayotte]] won the open seat by over 23 points, after winning a close party primary. New Hampshire trended Democratic in the 2006 and 2008 elections, with Republican incumbents losing both of the state's [[United States House of Representatives|House]] seats and its other [[United States Senate|Senate]] seat to Democrats, but polling conducted in late December 2008 showed Gregg defeating both of the state's U.S. Representatives in a hypothetical match-up.

Democrat [[Paul Hodes]] called himself a fiscal conservative, which was mocked by Republican [[Kelly Ayotte]] in a TV ad. Hodes was criticized for supporting President Obama's [[American Recovery and Reinvestment Act|Economic Recovery]] package, a [[cap and trade|carbon energy tax]], and [[Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act|Affordable Care Act]].<ref>{{Cite web |title = Hodes gets prize for funniest TV ads - NashuaTelegraph.com  |url=http://www.nashuatelegraph.com/opinionletters/883808-263/hodes-gets-prizefor-funniest-tv-ads.html |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20160303184904/http://www.nashuatelegraph.com/opinionletters/883808-263/hodes-gets-prizefor-funniest-tv-ads.html |archive-date=March 3, 2016 |access-date=2010-10-24 |df=mdy-all}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |title=Dem Paul Hodes In NH-SEN TV Ad: I'm A 'Fiscal Conservative' Video |url=http://vodpod.com/watch/4465505-dem-paul-hodes-in-nh-sen-tv-ad-im-a-fiscal-conservative |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120403090854/http://vodpod.com/watch/4465505-dem-paul-hodes-in-nh-sen-tv-ad-im-a-fiscal-conservative |archive-date=April 3, 2012 |access-date=December 11, 2012 |publisher=Vodpod.com |df=mdy-all}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |last=Rayfield |first=Jillian |date=September 21, 2010 |title=Rove-Backed Group Attacks Hodes For 'Fiscal Conservative' Claim (VIDEO) {{!}} TPMDC |url=http://tpmdc.talkingpointsmemo.com/2010/09/american-crossroads-ad-attacks-hodes-for-fiscal-conservative-claim.php |access-date=December 11, 2012 |publisher=Tpmdc.talkingpointsmemo.com |archive-date=March 5, 2012 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120305184732/http://tpmdc.talkingpointsmemo.com/2010/09/american-crossroads-ad-attacks-hodes-for-fiscal-conservative-claim.php |url-status=dead }}</ref> Hodes criticized Ayotte for numerous controversies. One ad was questioned Ayotte's honesty in dealing with the Lakes Region Ponzi scheme which defrauded investors of almost $80 million.<ref>{{Cite web |title = Hodes launches 2nd attack ad against Ayotte - NashuaTelegraph.com  |url=http://www.nashuatelegraph.com/news/statenewengland/801156-227/hodes-launches-2nd-attack-ad-against-ayotte.html |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20160303185328/http://www.nashuatelegraph.com/news/statenewengland/801156-227/hodes-launches-2nd-attack-ad-against-ayotte.html |archive-date=March 3, 2016 |access-date=2010-10-24 |df=mdy-all}}</ref>

== New York ==
There were two elections, due to the resignation of [[Hillary Clinton]] in January 2009 to become the [[United States Secretary of State|U.S. Secretary of State]]. Both senate races, along with the [[2010 New York gubernatorial election|New York gubernatorial election]], one with a vulnerable Democratic incumbent and the other an open race, respectively,<ref>{{Cite news |last=Lovett |first=Kenneth |date=March 23, 2009 |title=New Siena Poll finds Gov. David Paterson's approval rating at just 19% |publisher=Nydailynews.com |location=New York |url=http://www.nydailynews.com/ny_local/2009/03/23/2009-03-23_new_siena_poll_finds_gov_david_patersons.html |access-date=June 14, 2010}}</ref> was believed to lead major New York Republicans to gravitate towards them rather than challenge the popular Schumer.<ref name="nydn020109">{{Cite news |last=Saltonsall |first=David |date=February 1, 2009 |title=Sen. Chuck Schumer unlikely to see competition in 2010 reelection bid |work=[[New York Daily News]] |url=https://www.nydailynews.com/news/politics/sen-chuck-schumer-competition-2010-reelection-bid-article-1.388907 |access-date=February 2, 2009}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |title=Senate Races 2010 |url=http://www.electoral-vote.com/evp2008/Senate/senate-2010.html |access-date=December 18, 2008 |publisher=VoteFromAbroad.org}}{{Dead link | date=October 2010 | bot=H3llBot}}</ref> As it happened, however, New York Republicans had difficulty drawing top-tier candidates to any of the three races.

=== New York (special) ===
{{Infobox election

| map_caption = County results<br/>'''Gillibrand:''' {{legend0|#a5b0ff|40–50%}} {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}} {{legend0|#584cde|70–80%}}  {{legend0|#3933e5|80–90%}}<br/>'''DioGuardi:''' {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}}

| title              = U.S. Senator
| before_election    = [[Kirsten Gillibrand]]
| before_party       = Democratic Party (US)
| after_election     = [[Kirsten Gillibrand]]<ref name="NYwon">AP, "Democratic Sens. Charles Schumer, Kirsten Gillibrand win N.Y. election: Both Democrats defeated underfunded, largely unknown Republicans. Charles Schumer won a third term while Kirsten Gillibrand will hold the seat she was appointed to last year." November 3, 2010. Found at [https://web.archive.org/web/20101105052457/http://www.latimes.com/news/politics/la-pn-ny-senate-20101102,0,2455851.story Los Angeles Times website]. Accessed November 3, 2010.</ref>
| after_party        = Democratic Party (US)

{{Main|2010 United States Senate special election in New York}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from New York}}
[[Governor of New York|Governor]] [[David Paterson]] had appointed Democrat [[Kirsten Gillibrand]] to serve as United States Senator this [[special election]], replacing former Senator [[Hillary Clinton]], who resigned to serve as [[United States Secretary of State|U.S. Secretary of State]] in the [[Barack Obama administration]]. The winner of the special election would complete the term ending in January 2013. Due to this special election, this was the first time since the [[ratification]] of the [[Seventeenth Amendment to the United States Constitution|17th Amendment]] in 1913 that all of New York's six statewide offices were up for popular election on the same date.

Gillibrand claimed to be an independent thinker who took a back seat to no one. She also released television advertising touting her background from [[upstate New York]]<ref>{{Cite web |title=Gillibrand, DioGuardi campaign in upstate NY – NewsChannel 9 WSYR<!-- Bot generated title --> |url=http://www.9wsyr.com/news/local/story/Gillibrand-DioGuardi-campaign-in-upstate-NY/A-vKKmao-k2TyKHefYu1sg.cspx |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120305233825/http://www.9wsyr.com/news/local/story/Gillibrand-DioGuardi-campaign-in-upstate-NY/A-vKKmao-k2TyKHefYu1sg.cspx |archive-date=March 5, 2012 |access-date=December 21, 2017 |df=mdy-all}}</ref> while DioGuardi criticized Gillibrand's appearance in ''[[Vogue magazine|Vogue]]'' magazine.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Barrett |first=Devlin |date=October 22, 2010 |title=Gillibrand, DioGuardi Spar in Debate |work=The Wall Street Journal |url=https://www.wsj.com/articles/SB10001424052702304023804575566952592140466?mod=googlenews_wsj}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |last=Raphael |first=Rina |title=Sen. Gillibrand's hot new D.C. look |website=[[NBC News]] |date=October 20, 2010 |url=https://www.nbcnews.com/id/wbna39759657 |url-status=live |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20101022174908/http://today.msnbc.msn.com/id/39759657/ns/today-today_fashion_and_beauty |archive-date=October 22, 2010 |access-date=December 21, 2017}}</ref>

== North Carolina ==
{{Infobox election
| election_name      = North Carolina election
| country            = North Carolina
| type               = presidential
| ongoing            = Yes
| previous_election  = 2004 United States Senate election in North Carolina
| previous_year      = 2004
| next_election      = 2016 United States Senate election in North Carolina
| next_year          = 2016
| image_size         = 125x136px

| image1             = Richard Burr official portrait crop.jpg
| nominee1           = '''[[Richard Burr]]'''
| party1             = Republican Party (US)
| popular_vote1      = '''1,458,046'''
| percentage1        = '''54.81%'''

| image2             = File:Elaine Marshall IACA 2018 (cropped).jpg
| nominee2           = [[Elaine Marshall]]
| party2             = Democratic Party (US)
| popular_vote2      = 1,145,074
| percentage2        = 43.05%

| map_image         = 2010 United States Senate election in North Carolina results map by county.svg
| map_size          = 300px
| map_caption = '''Burr:''' {{legend0|#ffb2b2|40–50%}} {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}}<br />'''Marshall:''' {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}} {{legend0|#584cde|70–80%}}

| title              = U.S. Senator
| before_election    = [[Richard Burr]]
| before_party       = Republican Party (US)
| after_election     = [[Richard Burr]]
| after_party        = Republican Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in North Carolina}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from North Carolina}}
Incumbent Republican [[Richard Burr]] won re-election to a second term. Burr was the first incumbent to win re-election for this seat since [[Sam J. Ervin, Jr.|Sam Ervin]]'s last re-election in [[1968 United States Senate election in North Carolina|1968]].<ref>[http://www.rasmussenreports.com/public_content/politics/elections2/election_2010/election_2010_senate_elections/north_carolina/election_2010_north_carolina_senate Election 2010: North Carolina Senate], [[Rasmussen Reports]], March 24, 2010.</ref> Burr's 54.8% also represented the highest vote share a North Carolina Republican received since the state began directly electing its senators.

This Senate seat was unfavorable to incumbents over the past several decades. No person elected to this seat was re-elected since [[Sam Ervin]] in [[1968 United States Senate elections|1968]]. His successor, Democrat [[Robert Burren Morgan]], was defeated for re-election in [[1980 United States Senate elections|1980]], along with many other incumbents from his party. His Republican successor, [[John Porter East]], committed suicide in 1986. East's appointed successor, [[Jim Broyhill]], served for just four months, resigning upon his November [[1986 United States Senate elections|1986]] election loss to former Democratic Governor [[Terry Sanford]]. In [[1992 United States Senate elections|1992]], the seat changed hands yet again, as Sanford was defeated by wealthy GOP businessman [[Lauch Faircloth]], who himself lost in his bid for a second term six years later by [[John Edwards]]. In [[2004 United States Senate elections|2004]], no incumbent was defeated, as Edwards was [[2004 United States presidential election|running for vice president]] and was not allowed to be on the ballot in both races. However, that year the seat did change parties for the fifth time in a row, with [[Richard Burr]] defeating Bill Clinton's onetime Chief of Staff [[Erskine Bowles]].

== North Dakota ==

| title              = U.S. Senator
| before_election    = [[Byron Dorgan]]
| before_party       = North Dakota Democratic–Nonpartisan League Party
| after_election     = [[John Hoeven]]
| after_party        = Republican Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in North Dakota}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from North Dakota}}
Incumbent Democrat [[Byron Dorgan]] did not seek re-election. Republican Governor [[John Hoeven]] won the open seat. Incumbent Byron Dorgan never had a difficult time getting elected, as he obtained 59%, 63%, and 68% in his three senate election bids, respectively. However, in December 2009, [[Rasmussen Reports]] conducted a hypothetical matchup of Governor [[John Hoeven]] against the incumbent. Hoeven led by a large margin, 58% to Dorgan's 36%. 61% of the state still had a favorable view of Dorgan, and if pitted against State Senator Duane Sand, the incumbent led 52% to 37%.<ref>{{Cite web |title=2010 North Dakota Senate: Hoeven 58%, Dorgan 36% - Rasmussen Reports |url=http://www.rasmussenreports.com/public_content/politics/elections/election_2010/election_2010_senate_elections/north_dakota/2010_north_dakota_senate_hoeven_58_dorgan_36 |access-date=August 21, 2010 |publisher=Rasmussenreports.com}}</ref>

Hoeven was challenged in the race by North Dakota State Senator [[Tracy Potter]] of [[Bismarck, North Dakota|Bismarck]]. Potter received the endorsement of the [[North Dakota Democratic-NPL Party]] at its state convention on March 27, 2010. Governor Hoeven and Senator Potter advanced to the November 2, 2010 general election following balloting in North Dakota's primary election, which was held June 8, 2010. Neither candidate faced any significant opposition in the primary election.

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Ohio}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Ohio}}
Incumbent Republican [[George Voinovich]] retired instead of seeking a third term.<ref name="retire">{{Cite web |last=Dubail |first=Jean |date=January 12, 2009 |title=It's official: Voinovich to retire from Senate after 2010 |url=http://blog.cleveland.com/openers/2009/01/its_official_voinovich_to_reti.html |access-date=November 10, 2009 |website=Cleveland Plain Dealer}}</ref> Republican former [[Director of the Office of Management and Budget]], [[United States Trade Representative]], and [[Ohio's 2nd congressional district|Congressman]] [[Rob Portman]] won the open seat.

{{Election box begin no change
 | title      = Democratic primary election<ref>{{Cite web |title=The 2010 Results Maps |url=https://www.politico.com/2010/maps/#/Senate/2010/OH |access-date=August 21, 2010 |publisher=Politico.Com |archive-date=August 23, 2010 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100823031212/http://www.politico.com/2010/maps/#/Senate/2010/OH |url-status=dead }}</ref>
}}

{{Election box begin no change

Originally, the election was seen as a toss-up.<ref>{{Cite web |title=CQ Politics - Poll Tracker - Ohio Senate: Portman Even With Fisher, Leads Brunner<!-- Bot generated title --> |url=http://blogs.cqpolitics.com/polltracker/2009/12/ohio-senate-portman-even-with.html |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20091214174652/http://blogs.cqpolitics.com/polltracker/2009/12/ohio-senate-portman-even-with.html |archive-date=December 14, 2009 |access-date=December 21, 2017 |df=mdy-all}}</ref> His experience as a former [[George W. Bush|Bush]] official was considered to be a big problem for Portman. Both President [[Barack Obama]] and Vice President [[Joseph Biden|Joe Biden]] campaigned for Fisher.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Prospects in Ohio Looking Better for Senate GOP : Roll Call<!-- Bot generated title --> |url=http://www.cqpolitics.com/wmspage.cfm?docID=news-000003728902 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100907075814/http://www.cqpolitics.com/wmspage.cfm?docid=news-000003728902 |archive-date=September 7, 2010 |access-date=December 21, 2017 |df=mdy-all}}</ref>  Television advertisements were very negative. Fisher attacked Portman for helping to ship jobs overseas during his entire political career, backing deals that shipped jobs overseas, and the trade deficit with China, which grew by over $41 billion.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Lee Fisher hammers Rob Portman on record in Bush administration |url=http://www.politifact.com/truth-o-meter/statements/2010/sep/09/lee-fisher/lee-fisher-hammers-rob-portman-record-bush-adminis |access-date=December 21, 2017}}</ref> Portman claimed in response that most jobs were being lost to other states, not countries.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Portman targets Fisher with claim that most jobs Ohio lost went to other states: PolitiFact Ohio |date=September 27, 2010 |url=http://www.cleveland.com/open/index.ssf/2010/09/portman_targets_fisher_with_cl.html |access-date=December 21, 2017}}</ref> Portman attacked Fisher for supporting Obama's [[American Reinvestment and Recovery Act|stimulus]] and [[Emissions trading|cap and trade]].<ref>{{Cite web |title=Democrats Use Portman Ad As Opening To Attack - The Eye (CQ Politics)<!-- Bot generated title --> |url=http://blogs.cqpolitics.com/eyeon2010/2010/07/senate-portman-cap-and-trade-a.html |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100716232121/http://blogs.cqpolitics.com/eyeon2010/2010/07/senate-portman-cap-and-trade-a.html |archive-date=July 16, 2010 |access-date=December 21, 2017 |df=mdy-all}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |title=Portman Hits Fisher on Jobs in New Ad - RealClearPolitics |url=http://www.realclearpolitics.com/articles/2010/09/29/portman_hits_fisher_on_jobs_in_new_ad_107361.html |access-date=December 21, 2017}}</ref>

Winning the election, Portman received the majority of votes in 82 of 88 counties and in 15 of 18 Congressional districts, including the district of liberal U.S. Congressman [[Dennis Kucinich]].<ref>{{Cite web |last1=Drucker |first1=David M. |date=March 15, 2011 |title=Portman Is GOP's Point Man in Ohio |url=https://www.rollcall.com/news/-204148-1.html |access-date=December 21, 2017 |website=Roll Call |archive-date=December 22, 2017 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20171222050759/https://www.rollcall.com/news/-204148-1.html |url-status=dead }}</ref>

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Oklahoma}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Oklahoma}}
Incumbent first-term Republican [[Tom Coburn]] won re-election to a second term. Coburn, a very popular incumbent, promised to limit himself to two terms. Despite his popularity, he did release television advertisements. In 2009, Coburn's approval rating in a [[Public Policy Polling|PPP]] poll was 59%, including a 39% approval rating among Democrats.<ref>http://www.publicpolicypolling.com/pdf/surveys/2009_Archives/PPP_Release_OK_519.pdf{{dead link|date=December 2017}}</ref> His Democratic opponent is a perennial candidate<ref>{{Cite web |title=Tulsa news, sports, business, entertainment, weather|url=http://www.tulsaworld.com/news/article.aspx |access-date=December 21, 2017 |website=Tulsa World}}</ref> who did little campaigning.<ref>{{Cite web |date=November 3, 2010 |title=Tom Coburn cruises to a second and final term in the U.S. Senate |url=http://newsok.com/tom-coburn-cruises-to-a-second-and-final-term-in-the-u.s.-senate/article/3510833#ixzz14WqGaxsU |access-date=December 21, 2017}}</ref>

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Oregon}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Oregon}}
Incumbent Democrat [[Ron Wyden]] won re-election to a third full term. Wyden, a popular incumbent with a 52% approval rating in a July poll,<ref>{{Cite web |title=SurveyUSA News Poll #16824 |url=http://www.surveyusa.com/client/PollReport.aspx?g=0b8dbb4e-b838-4e1e-9b6c-d6c5d794185c |access-date=December 21, 2017 |website=www.surveyusa.com}}</ref> touted bipartisanship and promised to hold town-hall meetings annually in each of Oregon's 36 counties and to open offices outside of [[Portland, Oregon|Portland]] and [[Salem, Oregon|Salem]].<ref>[http://www.statesmanjournal.com/article/20101028/NEWS/10280337/1001/news#ixzz14WlSG8xm Statesman Journal] {{dead link|date=December 2017}}</ref> A [[Survey USA]] poll taken a few days before the election showed that 23% of Republicans supported Wyden.<ref>{{Cite web |title=SurveyUSA Election Poll #17610 |url=http://www.surveyusa.com/client/PollReport.aspx?g=96e03446-a38c-4b68-aaa1-aa652fd9e342 |access-date=December 21, 2017 |website=www.surveyusa.com}}</ref>

Huffman, widely considered as an underdog, financed his own campaign. He defended bonuses for Wall Street executives and questioned [[global warming]].<ref>{{Cite news |last=Duara |first=Nigel |date=November 3, 2010 |title=Ore. Democrat holds Senate seat against professor |newspaper=The Washington Post |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/wp-dyn/content/article/2010/11/02/AR2010110208197.html}}</ref>

Incumbent Republican-turned-Democrat [[Arlen Specter]] ran for re-election to a sixth term,<ref name="cnn090428">{{Cite news |date=April 28, 2009 |title=Longtime GOP Sen. Republican nominee [[Pat Toomey]] then won the seat.

| before_election    = [[Jim DeMint]]
| before_party       = Republican Party (US)
| after_election     = [[Jim DeMint]]<ref>{{Cite news |date=November 2, 2010 |title=Jim DeMint Defeats Alvin Greene In South Carolina Senate Race |publisher=Huffingtonpost.com |url=http://www.huffingtonpost.com/2010/11/02/jim-demint-defeats-alvin-greene-south-carolina_n_767436.html |url-status=dead |access-date=January 24, 2011 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20101107161926/http://www.huffingtonpost.com/2010/11/02/jim-demint-defeats-alvin-greene-south-carolina_n_767436.html |archive-date=November 7, 2010 |df=mdy-all}}</ref>
| after_party        = Republican Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in South Carolina}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from South Carolina}}
Incumbent Republican [[Jim DeMint]] won re-election to a second term. [[Alvin Greene]], the Democratic nominee, was the first major-party [[African-American]] U.S. Senate candidate in South Carolina since [[Reconstruction era of the United States|Reconstruction]]. [[Alvin Greene]]'s Democratic primary election win and his margin of victory surprised pundits. As of the primary, he had held no public campaign events, raised no money, and did not have a campaign website. A review of the primary election showed that of the state's 46 counties, half had a significant gap between the absentee and primary day ballots. For example, in [[Lancaster County, South Carolina|Lancaster County]], Vic Rawl won the absentees with 84 percent, while Greene won primary day by a double-digit margin. Rawl's campaign manager also claimed, "In only two of 88 precincts, do the number of votes Greene got plus the number we got equal the total cast."<ref>{{Cite web |last=Catanese |first=David |date=June 11, 2010 |title=Experts review S.C. Senate ballots |url=https://www.politico.com/story/2010/06/experts-review-sc-senate-ballots-038433 |access-date=June 14, 2010 |publisher=Politico.Com}}</ref>

| after_election     = [[John Thune]]
| after_party        = Republican Party (US)

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in South Dakota}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from South Dakota}}
Incumbent Republican [[John Thune]] won re-election to a second term unopposed.<ref>{{Cite web |date=April 1, 2010 |title=Republican Sen. John Thune unopposed by Democrats |url=http://www.kcautv.com/Global/story.asp?S=12240983 |access-date=April 5, 2010 |publisher=[[KCAU-TV]]}}{{Dead link|date=July 2018 |bot=InternetArchiveBot |fix-attempted=yes }}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |date=April 29, 2010 |title=Kurt Evans drops out of SD race for US Senate |url=http://www.kcautv.com/global/story.asp?s=12400556 |access-date=June 8, 2010 |publisher=KCAU-TV}}{{Dead link|date=August 2018 |bot=InternetArchiveBot |fix-attempted=yes }}</ref>

Thune was narrowly elected to his first term over Democratic [[Senate Minority Leader]] [[Tom Daschle]] with 51% of the vote in [[2004 United States Senate election in South Dakota|2004]]. In spite of his lack of [[Seniority in the United States Senate|seniority]], Thune rose to the position of chairman of the [[Republican Policy Committee Chairman of the United States Senate|Republican Policy Committee]] in the United States Senate.

No members of the [[South Dakota Democratic Party]] (or any other party) filed to challenge Thune. [[Scott Heidepriem]], the [[South Dakota Senate]] Minority Leader and a Democratic [[2010 South Dakota gubernatorial election|candidate]] for [[Governor of South Dakota]], said, "We just concluded that John Thune is an extremely popular senator who is going to win another term in the Senate."<ref name="yankton">{{Cite web |last=Brokaw |first=Chet |date=April 1, 2010 |title=S.D. Dems Skip Senate Race Against GOP's Thune |url=http://www.yankton.net/articles/2010/04/02/news/doc4bb576acd9bc3030872173.txt |access-date=April 7, 2010 |website=Yankton Press & Dakotan}}</ref>

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Utah}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Utah}}
Incumbent Republican [[Bob Bennett (politician)|Bob Bennett]] was seeking re-election to a fourth term, but was eliminated in the GOP state convention. Republican [[Mike Lee]], who won the Republican primary, won the open seat.

At the Republican convention, incumbent Senator Bob Bennett finished third in balloting among delegates and was eliminated from the race. Business owner Tim Bridgewater finished first and attorney Mike Lee finished second, but Bridgewater did not receive enough votes to avoid a primary election runoff against Lee.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Gehrke |first=Robert |title=Bennett out; GOP delegates reject 18-year Senate veteran - Salt Lake Tribune |url=http://www.sltrib.com/news/ci_15046264 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100511133016/http://www.sltrib.com/news/ci_15046264 |archive-date=May 11, 2010 |access-date=June 14, 2010 |publisher=Sltrib.com |df=mdy-all}}</ref> At the Democratic convention, delegates nominated businessman Sam Granato, who received 77.5 percent of the vote.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Loomis |first=Brandon |title=Matheson forced into runoff election - Salt Lake Tribune |url=http://www.sltrib.com/news/ci_15046643 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20100512154711/http://www.sltrib.com/news/ci_15046643 |archive-date=May 12, 2010 |access-date=June 14, 2010 |publisher=Sltrib.com}}</ref>

{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Washington}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Washington}}
Incumbent Democrat [[Patty Murray]] won re-election to a fourth term.

Republican Dino Rossi heavily criticized Murray for her support of the [[American Recovery and Reinvestment Act|2009 economic stimulus package]]; however, Rossi's economic promises are nearly identical to those of President Bush who asked for the stimulus.<ref>{{Cite web |title=The Stimulus 18-Month Check Up: Murray Plan Means Big Debt, Few Jobs - Dino Rossi for Senate<!-- Bot generated title --> |url=http://www.dinorossi.com/the-stimulus-18-month-check-up-murray-plan-means-big-debt-few-jobs |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20101111191726/http://www.dinorossi.com/the-stimulus-18-month-check-up-murray-plan-means-big-debt-few-jobs/ |archive-date=November 11, 2010 |access-date=December 21, 2017 |df=mdy-all}}</ref> Rossi supports repealing the [[Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act]] and the [[Dodd-Frank Wall Street Reform and Consumer Protection Act]]. He also criticized Murray for her support for [[Earmark (politics)|earmarks]]. In response, Murray said, "You bet that seniority and leadership has a big thing to do with it, but the other part of it is, I get up every day and I work hard and I believe in this and I am going to continue fighting for the community I represent."<ref>{{Cite news |last=Brunner |first=Jim |date=August 7, 2010 |title=Murray touts bringing home the bucks |work=The Seattle Times |url=http://seattletimes.nwsource.com/html/localnews/2012565369_murray08m.html}}</ref>

{{Main|2010 United States Senate special election in West Virginia}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from West Virginia}}
Long-time Democratic Senator [[Robert Byrd]] died June 28, 2010, and Democratic Governor [[Joe Manchin]] appointed [[Carte Goodwin]] to temporarily fill the vacancy. Goodwin pledged to not run for election to the seat in exchange for the appointment.  Manchin won the open seat and served out the remainder of Byrd's elected term, which ended on January 3, 2013.<ref>{{Cite news |date=November 2, 2010 |title=Democrat Manchin wins key W. Va. Senate race |work=[[Chicago Sun-Times]] |agency=Associated Press |url=http://www.suntimes.com/news/elections/2858740,manchin-west-virginia-senate-110210.article}}{{Dead link|date=July 2018 |bot=InternetArchiveBot |fix-attempted=yes }}</ref>

During the Republican primary campaign, only Raese and Warner released television advertisements.<ref>{{Cite web |date=August 17, 2010 |title=Special Election Ads Slow to Start - WOWK-TV |url=http://wowktv.com/story.cfm?func=viewstory&storyid=84585 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20110726080907/http://wowktv.com/story.cfm?func=viewstory&storyid=84585 |archive-date=July 26, 2011 |access-date=August 21, 2010 |publisher=WOWKTV.com |df=mdy-all}}</ref>

| before_election    = [[Russ Feingold]]
| before_party       = Democratic Party (US)
| after_election     = [[Ron Johnson]]
| after_party        = Republican Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2010 United States Senate election in Wisconsin}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Wisconsin}}
Incumbent Democrat [[Russ Feingold]] ran for re-election to a fourth term, but was defeated by political newcomer, Republican [[Ron Johnson]].  Johnson, a millionaire manufacturer and [[Tea Party movement]] favorite who was running for his first political office, was the front runner. In response to controversy over his ownership of stock in [[BP]], Johnson said he would sell it when market conditions were favorable and possibly use the proceeds to help finance his Senate campaign.  Feingold's first television ad was a positive ad released in March. In July 2010, Feingold's second 2010 television election ad attacked Johnson for alleged support for [[offshore drilling]] in the [[Great Lakes]]. Johnson quickly countered Feingold with a television ad of his own. Feingold's logo was ''Moving Forward''. In one ad he emphasized independence and called himself a "[[Frugality|Penny Pincher]]."<ref>{{Cite web |last1=Walker |first1=Don |title=Washington-based group airs ad attacking Feingold |url=http://www.jsonline.com/news/statepolitics/101409589.html |access-date=December 21, 2017 |website=www.jsonline.com}}</ref> Johnson argued that his professions, [[manufacturing|manufacturer]] and [[accountant]] were underrepresented in the U.S. Senate, and there were too many lawyers (57 out of 100 members), including Feingold.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Ron Johnson says there are 57 lawyers, including Russ Feingold, in the U.S. Senate |url=http://www.politifact.com/wisconsin/statements/2010/oct/22/ron-johnson/ron-johnson-says-there-are-57-lawyers-including-ru |access-date=December 21, 2017}}</ref>
